  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F22, F23.3, F06.2, F15.5, F19.5\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m here because things at the warehouse aren’t right. The routing system, the one they use for the trucks and scanners, it’s not just a computer thing anymore. It’s been marking my moves, like keeping track of where I go, and the scanners send these little messages… not regular words exactly, but I know what they mean.\n\nThey’re trying to push me out because I’m one of the old hands there. I’ve been around long enough to see how the place changed—used to be you knew everybody by name, now it’s all screens and beeps and some kid telling you the system says this or that. But the system’s been singling me out, and that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you used methamphetamine, cocaine, or any other street drugs recently?\n\nPatient: Uh… street drugs? No, I mean, not like that. I don’t mess with meth or cocaine or anything. I’ve seen what that stuff did to a guy I worked with years back—he’d come in all wired and start arguing with the pallet jack like it insulted him, you know? Management didn’t care until he slowed the line down.\n\nBut me, no. I’m just trying to keep my head down and do my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not all the time, but it happens. Like even when I’m sitting in my garage, and everything’s shut off — no radio, no scanner, no TV — I’ll hear dispatch voices clear as day.\n\nThey’ll call my operator number, like I’m back on the floor, and say things like I’m dangerous, or that security’s coming for me. And the garage has that old freezer in it, it hums sometimes, but this isn’t that. I know what a machine sounds like. I’ve worked around equipment my whole life. This is voices.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt as if someone or something is putting thoughts into your mind that are not your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s exactly thoughts being put in, like… not in the movie way. But the routing system at work, it does get in my head. It marks where I go, and then the scanners send these little messages, and I know they’re meant for me.\n\nIt’s like it wants me thinking a certain way — like I’m the problem, like I should leave — because I’ve been there a long time and I know how things used to be before all those screens took over. People used to just talk to each other, you know? Now it’s all beeps and numbers, and somehow it all points back at me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your thoughts were being broadcast so that other people could know what you were thinking?\n\nPatient: Broadcast, like everybody can hear it? I don’t know if it’s exactly like that… not like a radio station coming out of my head.\n\nBut at the warehouse, it feels close. The routing system isn’t just a computer to me anymore. It’s marking where I go, what I’m doing, and then those scanners send messages through, like they’re aimed right at me. Makes it seem like people already know I’m the one they’re watching, like they’re trying to push me out because I’m one of the old hands there.\n\nI mean, I’ve been around long enough to know when a system is just sorting pallets and when it’s… singling you out.\n\nDoctor: Before these experiences started, did you have any major medical problem or head injury?\n\nPatient: I mean… not that I can point to, no. I’ve had the usual knocks and aches from work, you know, climbing in and out of trucks, loading areas, all that concrete on your feet all day. But a major head injury? I don’t think so.\n\nMedical stuff… I’d have to think. Nothing that I remember as being the thing that started all this. It wasn’t like I fell and woke up different, if that’s what you mean. It was more… things at work changed over time, and everybody acts like that doesn’t matter, but when you’ve been around machines long enough, you notice when the rhythm is off.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself losing interest in things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s funny, because I used to have things that would kind of settle me down. Fishing, especially. I’d keep my rods ready, and choir practice too, and I used to mess around with wood out in the garage, little projects, sanding and measuring and all that.\n\nNow I can look right at the rods or the tools and it’s like… there’s no pull there. I know I used to care about it, but I don’t feel that “go do it” feeling. The stuff is just sitting there.\n\nDoctor: How long have these voices and the feeling that the warehouse system is targeting you been going on?\n\nPatient: It’s been… longer than I like to admit. About a year and eight months, I’d say, with the voices and that feeling about the scanners, the routing system watching me.\n\nIt didn’t just flip on one day, you know. At first I thought it was just a bad week at work, new equipment, people acting strange. Then it kept going, and you start counting time by shifts and pay periods, not calendars. But yeah… about a year and eight months.\n\nDoctor: When was the last time you used amphetamines, meth, cocaine, or any other non-prescribed drug?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that exactly. I’m not… I’m not keeping a calendar of that kind of thing, and I don’t want to say something wrong.\n\nI mean, people at work talk, you know, there’s always somebody who knows somebody, especially around long shifts and loading docks. Guys will say they need something to stay awake, then next thing they’re borrowing money or disappearing for two days. I always tried to keep myself apart from that, because once people know your business, it gets around fast.\n\nSo… I don’t have a clear “last time” to give you. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices still happened during a full month when you were not using any non-prescribed drugs at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out that cleanly. A whole month is a long time to look back on, and I don’t really keep track like that.\n\nThere were stretches where I wasn’t around any of that stuff, I think, but then work gets mixed in, people talking, the machines beeping, all the noise in the warehouse… it all kind of runs together. I don’t want to tell you yes or no and have it be wrong.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a full month when you were not using any non-prescribed drugs at all, and the voices still happened?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that cleanly, like “this month yes, that month no.” My life wasn’t organized that way. It was more like shifts, overtime, going home tired, trying to sleep, then back again.\n\nThere were times I wasn’t around people using anything, sure, but whether that was a full month… I can’t promise that. And the voices, they weren’t like something I was logging down in a notebook. I’d be at work hearing forklifts back up and the intercom crackle, and then I’d start wondering what was meant for me and what wasn’t.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… I’m not sure. I don’t want to say yes if I can’t really prove it.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices first start while you were using non-prescribed drugs, or soon after using them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, I hear what you’re asking, like which came first, but I can’t line it up that neatly.\n\nBack then everything was kind of mixed together — work, long hours, people talking, me not sleeping right, machines making noise all day. And people would say things about drugs like it was just part of getting through a shift, but I can’t say, “yes, that’s when it started,” or “no, it wasn’t that.”\n\nI don’t want to give you an answer that sounds certain when I’m not certain. I just… I don’t remember it in that order.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech has become hard to follow or jumbled?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe. People tell me to slow down sometimes, or they’ll say, “What are you talking about?” But that could just be because they weren’t listening, too.\n\nAt work, you know, with the radios and the backup alarms and everybody rushing, nobody follows anything unless you say it twice. My supervisor used to act like I was the one not making sense, but he’d give instructions half-mumbled while walking away, so I don’t know who was hard to follow.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if they meant my speech was jumbled, exactly. Maybe they’ve said I jump around a bit.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and eight months, was there ever a time when the voices and the feeling that the system was targeting you were happening even though you were not feeling depressed or unusually high?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There was a stretch where I wasn’t as down as I had been. I was eating a little better, talking more, not just sitting there quiet all the time.\n\nBut even then, for a few weeks, I was still hearing dispatch like they were calling my number, and I still thought the routing system was keeping tabs on me. The routes, the numbers, the timing of it… it felt like it was aimed at me.\n\nAnd that’s the thing with those systems, they act like it’s all just computers and schedules, but when you’ve driven around enough places you start seeing how the numbers line up. So, yeah, even when my mood wasn’t so bad, that part was still happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you seen things that other people could not see?\n\nPatient: Seen things? Not so much like… I don’t know, shadows or people standing there, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll be sitting in my garage, everything shut off — no radio, no truck noise, nothing running — and I still hear dispatch clear as day. Like they’re calling my operator number, saying I’m dangerous, or saying security is coming.\n\nAnd the garage has this funny echo anyway, because of the concrete and the way sound comes through from the street, so I’ll check sometimes, but… it’s not like anybody’s actually there. So seeing, no, not really. Hearing, yeah, that’s the part.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices talking to each other about you, as if two or more people are discussing you when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes it sounds like dispatch, like more than one voice on the radio, but there’s no radio on. I’ll be sitting in my garage with everything shut off, and I still hear them clear, calling my operator number.\n\nThey’ll say I’m dangerous, or that security’s coming for me. It’s like they’re talking about me through the system. And the garage, it carries sound weird, you know, concrete walls and all that, but when everything’s off… I still hear it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that an outside force is controlling your thoughts or actions?\n\nPatient: Controlled… I don’t know if I’d say like a puppet, exactly. I still move my own hands, drive my own route, all that.\n\nBut the routing system at the warehouse, that thing isn’t just a computer to me anymore. It marks where I go, what scanner I use, what time I hit a dock. And then the messages come through the scanners like they’re nudging me, pushing me out, because I’m one of the old hands there.\n\nThey always say it’s just scheduling, just logistics, but I’ve been around long enough to know when something’s aimed at me. So… not controlling every thought maybe, but it’s steering things around me. Trying to force my hand.\n\nDoctor: During the past year and eight months, have you been using methamphetamine regularly?\n\nPatient: Meth? No, I don’t… I mean, I’ve seen what that stuff does to guys on the yard, especially on long shifts, everybody trying to stay awake and then they’re grinding their teeth by lunch.\n\nBut me using it regularly, no. That’s not something I’ve been doing. I drink coffee, too much probably, the gas station kind that tastes burnt, but meth isn’t my thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I’m just trying to keep my head down and do my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not all the time, but it happens. It marks where I go, and then the scanners send these little messages, and I know they’re meant for me.\n\nIt’s like it wants me thinking a certain way — like I’m the problem, like I should leave — because I’ve been there a long time and I know how things used to be before all those screens took over. People used to just talk to each other, you know? Now it’s all beeps and numbers, and somehow it all points back at me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your thoughts were being broadcast so that other people could know what you were thinking?\n\nPatient: Broadcast, like everybody can hear it? I don’t know if it’s exactly like that… not like a radio station coming out of my head.\n\nBut at the warehouse, it feels close. The routing system isn’t just a computer to me anymore. It’s marking where I go, what I’m doing, and then those scanners send messages through, like they’re aimed right at me. Makes it seem like people already know I’m the one they’re watching, like they’re trying to push me out because I’m one of the old hands there.\n\nI mean, I’ve been around long enough to know when a system is just sorting pallets and when it’s… singling you out.\n\nDoctor: Before these experiences started, did you have any major medical problem or head injury?\n\nPatient: I mean… not that I can point to, no. I’ve had the usual knocks and aches from work, you know, climbing in and out of trucks, loading areas, all that concrete on your feet all day. But a major head injury? I don’t think so.\n\nMedical stuff… I’d have to think. Nothing that I remember as being the thing that started all this. It wasn’t like I fell and woke up different, if that’s what you mean. It was more… things at work changed over time, and everybody acts like that doesn’t matter, but when you’ve been around machines long enough, you notice when the rhythm is off.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself losing interest in things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s funny, because I used to have things that would kind of settle me down. Fishing, especially. I’d keep my rods ready, and choir practice too, and I used to mess around with wood out in the garage, little projects, sanding and measuring and all that.\n\nNow I can look right at the rods or the tools and it’s like… there’s no pull there. I know I used to care about it, but I don’t feel that “go do it” feeling. The stuff is just sitting there.\n\nDoctor: How long have these voices and the feeling that the warehouse system is targeting you been going on?\n\nPatient: It’s been… longer than I like to admit. About a year and eight months, I’d say, with the voices and that feeling about the scanners, the routing system watching me.\n\nIt didn’t just flip on one day, you know. At first I thought it was just a bad week at work, new equipment, people acting strange. Then it kept going, and you start counting time by shifts and pay periods, not calendars. But yeah… about a year and eight months.\n\nDoctor: When was the last time you used amphetamines, meth, cocaine, or any other non-prescribed drug?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that exactly. I’m not… I’m not keeping a calendar of that kind of thing, and I don’t want to say something wrong.\n\nI mean, people at work talk, you know, there’s always somebody who knows somebody, especially around long shifts and loading docks. Guys will say they need something to stay awake, then next thing they’re borrowing money or disappearing for two days. I always tried to keep myself apart from that, because once people know your business, it gets around fast.\n\nSo… I don’t have a clear “last time” to give you. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices still happened during a full month when you were not using any non-prescribed drugs at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out that cleanly. A whole month is a long time to look back on, and I don’t really keep track like that.\n\nThere were stretches where I wasn’t around any of that stuff, I think, but then work gets mixed in, people talking, the machines beeping, all the noise in the warehouse… it all kind of runs together. I don’t want to tell you yes or no and have it be wrong.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a full month when you were not using any non-prescribed drugs at all, and the voices still happened?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that cleanly, like “this month yes, that month no.” My life wasn’t organized that way. It was more like shifts, overtime, going home tired, trying to sleep, then back again.\n\nThere were times I wasn’t around people using anything, sure, but whether that was a full month… I can’t promise that. And the voices, they weren’t like something I was logging down in a notebook. I’d be at work hearing forklifts back up and the intercom crackle, and then I’d start wondering what was meant for me and what wasn’t.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… I’m not sure. I don’t want to say yes if I can’t really prove it.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices first start while you were using non-prescribed drugs, or soon after using them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, I hear what you’re asking, like which came first, but I can’t line it up that neatly.\n\nBack then everything was kind of mixed together — work, long hours, people talking, me not sleeping right, machines making noise all day. I just… I don’t remember it in that order.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech has become hard to follow or jumbled?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe. People tell me to slow down sometimes, or they’ll say, “What are you talking about?” But that could just be because they weren’t listening, too.\n\nAt work, you know, with the radios and the backup alarms and everybody rushing, nobody follows anything unless you say it twice. My supervisor used to act like I was the one not making sense, but he’d give instructions half-mumbled while walking away, so I don’t know who was hard to follow.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if they meant my speech was jumbled, exactly. Maybe they’ve said I jump around a bit.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and eight months, was there ever a time when the voices and the feeling that the system was targeting you were happening even though you were not feeling depressed or unusually high?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There was a stretch where I wasn’t as down as I had been. I was eating a little better, talking more, not just sitting there quiet all the time.\n\nBut even then, for a few weeks, I was still hearing dispatch like they were calling my number, and I still thought the routing system was keeping tabs on me. The routes, the numbers, the timing of it… it felt like it was aimed at me.\n\nAnd that’s the thing with those systems, they act like it’s all just computers and schedules, but when you’ve driven around enough places you start seeing how the numbers line up. So, yeah, even when my mood wasn’t so bad, that part was still happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you seen things that other people could not see?\n\nPatient: Seen things? Not so much like… I don’t know, shadows or people standing there, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll be sitting in my garage, everything shut off — no radio, no truck noise, nothing running — and I still hear dispatch clear as day. Like they’re calling my operator number, saying I’m dangerous, or saying security is coming.\n\nAnd the garage has this funny echo anyway, because of the concrete and the way sound comes through from the street, so I’ll check sometimes, but… it’s not like anybody’s actually there. So seeing, no, not really. Hearing, yeah, that’s the part.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices talking to each other about you, as if two or more people are discussing you when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes it sounds like dispatch, like more than one voice on the radio, but there’s no radio on. I’m just trying to keep my head down and do my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not all the time, but it happens. Like even when I’m sitting in my garage, and everything’s shut off — no radio, no scanner, no TV — I’ll hear dispatch voices clear as day.\n\nThey’ll call my operator number, like I’m back on the floor, and say things like I’m dangerous, or that security’s coming for me. And the garage has that old freezer in it, it hums sometimes, but this isn’t that. I know what a machine sounds like. I’ve worked around equipment my whole life. This is voices.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt as if someone or something is putting thoughts into your mind that are not your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s exactly thoughts being put in, like… not in the movie way. But the routing system at work, it does get in my head. It marks where I go, and then the scanners send these little messages, and I know they’re meant for me.\n\nIt’s like it wants me thinking a certain way — like I’m the problem, like I should leave — because I’ve been there a long time and I know how things used to be before all those screens took over. People used to just talk to each other, you know? Now it’s all beeps and numbers, and somehow it all points back at me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your thoughts were being broadcast so that other people could know what you were thinking?\n\nPatient: Broadcast, like everybody can hear it? I don’t know if it’s exactly like that… not like a radio station coming out of my head.\n\nBut at the warehouse, it feels close. The routing system isn’t just a computer to me anymore. It’s marking where I go, what I’m doing, and then those scanners send messages through, like they’re aimed right at me. Makes it seem like people already know I’m the one they’re watching, like they’re trying to push me out because I’m one of the old hands there.\n\nI mean, I’ve been around long enough to know when a system is just sorting pallets and when it’s… singling you out.\n\nDoctor: Before these experiences started, did you have any major medical problem or head injury?\n\nPatient: I mean… not that I can point to, no. I’ve had the usual knocks and aches from work, you know, climbing in and out of trucks, loading areas, all that concrete on your feet all day. But a major head injury? I don’t think so.\n\nMedical stuff… I’d have to think. Nothing that I remember as being the thing that started all this. It wasn’t like I fell and woke up different, if that’s what you mean. It was more… things at work changed over time, and everybody acts like that doesn’t matter, but when you’ve been around machines long enough, you notice when the rhythm is off.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself losing interest in things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s funny, because I used to have things that would kind of settle me down. Fishing, especially. I’d keep my rods ready, and choir practice too, and I used to mess around with wood out in the garage, little projects, sanding and measuring and all that.\n\nNow I can look right at the rods or the tools and it’s like… there’s no pull there. I know I used to care about it, but I don’t feel that “go do it” feeling. The stuff is just sitting there.\n\nDoctor: How long have these voices and the feeling that the warehouse system is targeting you been going on?\n\nPatient: It’s been… longer than I like to admit. About a year and eight months, I’d say, with the voices and that feeling about the scanners, the routing system watching me.\n\nIt didn’t just flip on one day, you know. At first I thought it was just a bad week at work, new equipment, people acting strange. Then it kept going, and you start counting time by shifts and pay periods, not calendars. But yeah… about a year and eight months.\n\nDoctor: When was the last time you used amphetamines, meth, cocaine, or any other non-prescribed drug?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that exactly. I’m not… I’m not keeping a calendar of that kind of thing, and I don’t want to say something wrong.\n\nI mean, people at work talk, you know, there’s always somebody who knows somebody, especially around long shifts and loading docks. Guys will say they need something to stay awake, then next thing they’re borrowing money or disappearing for two days. I always tried to keep myself apart from that, because once people know your business, it gets around fast.\n\nSo… I don’t have a clear “last time” to give you. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices still happened during a full month when you were not using any non-prescribed drugs at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out that cleanly. A whole month is a long time to look back on, and I don’t really keep track like that.\n\nThere were stretches where I wasn’t around any of that stuff, I think, but then work gets mixed in, people talking, the machines beeping, all the noise in the warehouse… it all kind of runs together. I don’t want to tell you yes or no and have it be wrong.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a full month when you were not using any non-prescribed drugs at all, and the voices still happened?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that cleanly, like “this month yes, that month no.” My life wasn’t organized that way. It was more like shifts, overtime, going home tired, trying to sleep, then back again.\n\nThere were times I wasn’t around people using anything, sure, but whether that was a full month… I can’t promise that. And the voices, they weren’t like something I was logging down in a notebook. I’d be at work hearing forklifts back up and the intercom crackle, and then I’d start wondering what was meant for me and what wasn’t.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… I’m not sure. I don’t want to say yes if I can’t really prove it.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices first start while you were using non-prescribed drugs, or soon after using them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, I hear what you’re asking, like which came first, but I can’t line it up that neatly.\n\nBack then everything was kind of mixed together — work, long hours, people talking, me not sleeping right, machines making noise all day. And people would say things about drugs like it was just part of getting through a shift, but I can’t say, “yes, that’s when it started,” or “no, it wasn’t that.”\n\nI don’t want to give you an answer that sounds certain when I’m not certain. I just… I don’t remember it in that order.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech has become hard to follow or jumbled?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe. People tell me to slow down sometimes, or they’ll say, “What are you talking about?” But that could just be because they weren’t listening, too.\n\nAt work, you know, with the radios and the backup alarms and everybody rushing, nobody follows anything unless you say it twice. My supervisor used to act like I was the one not making sense, but he’d give instructions half-mumbled while walking away, so I don’t know who was hard to follow.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if they meant my speech was jumbled, exactly. Maybe they’ve said I jump around a bit.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and eight months, was there ever a time when the voices and the feeling that the system was targeting you were happening even though you were not feeling depressed or unusually high?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There was a stretch where I wasn’t as down as I had been. I was eating a little better, talking more, not just sitting there quiet all the time.\n\nBut even then, for a few weeks, I was still hearing dispatch like they were calling my number, and I still thought the routing system was keeping tabs on me. The routes, the numbers, the timing of it… it felt like it was aimed at me.\n\nAnd that’s the thing with those systems, they act like it’s all just computers and schedules, but when you’ve driven around enough places you start seeing how the numbers line up. So, yeah, even when my mood wasn’t so bad, that part was still happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you seen things that other people could not see?\n\nPatient: Seen things? Not so much like… I don’t know, shadows or people standing there, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll be sitting in my garage, everything shut off — no radio, no truck noise, nothing running — and I still hear dispatch clear as day. Like they’re calling my operator number, saying I’m dangerous, or saying security is coming.\n\nAnd the garage has this funny echo anyway, because of the concrete and the way sound comes through from the street, so I’ll check sometimes, but… it’s not like anybody’s actually there. So seeing, no, not really. Hearing, yeah, that’s the part.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices talking to each other about you, as if two or more people are discussing you when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes it sounds like dispatch, like more than one voice on the radio, but there’s no radio on. I’ll be sitting in my garage with everything shut off, and I still hear them clear, calling my operator number.\n\nThey’ll say I’m dangerous, or that security’s coming for me. It’s like they’re talking about me through the system. And the garage, it carries sound weird, you know, concrete walls and all that, but when everything’s off… I still hear it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that an outside force is controlling your thoughts or actions?\n\nPatient: Controlled… I don’t know if I’d say like a puppet, exactly. I still move my own hands, drive my own route, all that.\n\nBut the routing system at the warehouse, that thing isn’t just a computer to me anymore. It marks where I go, what scanner I use, what time I hit a dock. And then the messages come through the scanners like they’re nudging me, pushing me out, because I’m one of the old hands there.\n\nThey always say it’s just scheduling, just logistics, but I’ve been around long enough to know when something’s aimed at me. So… not controlling every thought maybe, but it’s steering things around me. Trying to force my hand.\n\nDoctor: During the past year and eight months, have you been using methamphetamine regularly?\n\nPatient: Meth? No, I don’t… I mean, I’ve seen what that stuff does to guys on the yard, especially on long shifts, everybody trying to stay awake and then they’re grinding their teeth by lunch.\n\nBut me using it regularly, no. That’s not something I’ve been doing. I drink coffee, too much probably, the gas station kind that tastes burnt, but meth isn’t my thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear a voice speaking your thoughts out loud as you think them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s my thoughts exactly, like word-for-word while I’m thinking them. It’s more… I’ll be sitting in my garage, everything shut off, no radio, no scanner, and I still hear dispatch voices clear as day.\n\nThey call my operator number, like they’re logged into the system or something, and then they’ll say I’m dangerous, or that security’s coming for me. Yeah… mixed up, yes. It’s not just forgetting what I walked into a room for, like everybody does. At work especially, I’ll sort of freeze there and my hands… I’ll be holding them like they’re still on the forklift controls, even if I’m not on it.

It happens at home some too. I’ll be by the workbench or in the kitchen and just… lose the order of things. Like the steps won’t line up right. Then I’m back thinking about the yard, the docks, the old warning signs they used to have before they repainted everything—sorry, I know that’s not what you asked. But yes, my thoughts get mixed up enough that I can’t keep them straight.

People used to just talk to each other, you know? Now it’s all beeps and numbers, and somehow it all points back at me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your thoughts were being broadcast so that other people could know what you were thinking?\n\nPatient: Broadcast, like everybody can hear it? I don’t know if it’s exactly like that… not like a radio station coming out of my head.\n\nBut at the warehouse, it feels close. The routing system isn’t just a computer to me anymore. It’s marking where I go, what I’m doing, and then those scanners send messages through, like they’re aimed right at me. Makes it seem like people already know I’m the one they’re watching, like they’re trying to push me out because I’m one of the old hands there.\n\nI mean, I’ve been around long enough to know when a system is just sorting pallets and when it’s… singling you out.\n\nDoctor: Before these experiences started, did you have any major medical problem or head injury?\n\nPatient: I mean… not that I can point to, no. I’ve had the usual knocks and aches from work, you know, climbing in and out of trucks, loading areas, all that concrete on your feet all day. But a major head injury? I don’t think so.\n\nMedical stuff… I’d have to think. Nothing that I remember as being the thing that started all this. It wasn’t like I fell and woke up different, if that’s what you mean. It was more… things at work changed over time, and everybody acts like that doesn’t matter, but when you’ve been around machines long enough, you notice when the rhythm is off.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself losing interest in things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s funny, because I used to have things that would kind of settle me down. Fishing, especially. I’d keep my rods ready, and choir practice too, and I used to mess around with wood out in the garage, little projects, sanding and measuring and all that.\n\nNow I can look right at the rods or the tools and it’s like… there’s no pull there. I know I used to care about it, but I don’t feel that “go do it” feeling. The stuff is just sitting there.\n\nDoctor: How long have these voices and the feeling that the warehouse system is targeting you been going on?\n\nPatient: It’s been… longer than I like to admit. About a year and eight months, I’d say, with the voices and that feeling about the scanners, the routing system watching me.\n\nIt didn’t just flip on one day, you know. At first I thought it was just a bad week at work, new equipment, people acting strange. Then it kept going, and you start counting time by shifts and pay periods, not calendars. But yeah… about a year and eight months.\n\nDoctor: When was the last time you used amphetamines, meth, cocaine, or any other non-prescribed drug?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that exactly. I’m not… I’m not keeping a calendar of that kind of thing, and I don’t want to say something wrong.\n\nI mean, people at work talk, you know, there’s always somebody who knows somebody, especially around long shifts and loading docks. Guys will say they need something to stay awake, then next thing they’re borrowing money or disappearing for two days. I always tried to keep myself apart from that, because once people know your business, it gets around fast.\n\nSo… I don’t have a clear “last time” to give you. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices still happened during a full month when you were not using any non-prescribed drugs at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out that cleanly. A whole month is a long time to look back on, and I don’t really keep track like that.\n\nThere were stretches where I wasn’t around any of that stuff, I think, but then work gets mixed in, people talking, the machines beeping, all the noise in the warehouse… it all kind of runs together. I don’t want to tell you yes or no and have it be wrong.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a full month when you were not using any non-prescribed drugs at all, and the voices still happened?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that cleanly, like “this month yes, that month no.” My life wasn’t organized that way. It was more like shifts, overtime, going home tired, trying to sleep, then back again.\n\nThere were times I wasn’t around people using anything, sure, but whether that was a full month… I can’t promise that. And the voices, they weren’t like something I was logging down in a notebook. I’d be at work hearing forklifts back up and the intercom crackle, and then I’d start wondering what was meant for me and what wasn’t.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… I’m not sure. I don’t want to say yes if I can’t really prove it.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices first start while you were using non-prescribed drugs, or soon after using them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, I hear what you’re asking, like which came first, but I can’t line it up that neatly.\n\nBack then everything was kind of mixed together — work, long hours, people talking, me not sleeping right, machines making noise all day. And people would say things about drugs like it was just part of getting through a shift, but I can’t say, “yes, that’s when it started,” or “no, it wasn’t that.”\n\nI don’t want to give you an answer that sounds certain when I’m not certain. I just… I don’t remember it in that order.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech has become hard to follow or jumbled?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe. People tell me to slow down sometimes, or they’ll say, “What are you talking about?” But that could just be because they weren’t listening, too.\n\nAt work, you know, with the radios and the backup alarms and everybody rushing, nobody follows anything unless you say it twice. My supervisor used to act like I was the one not making sense, but he’d give instructions half-mumbled while walking away, so I don’t know who was hard to follow.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if they meant my speech was jumbled, exactly. Maybe they’ve said I jump around a bit.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and eight months, was there ever a time when the voices and the feeling that the system was targeting you were happening even though you were not feeling depressed or unusually high?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There was a stretch where I wasn’t as down as I had been. I was eating a little better, talking more, not just sitting there quiet all the time.\n\nBut even then, for a few weeks, I was still hearing dispatch like they were calling my number, and I still thought the routing system was keeping tabs on me. The routes, the numbers, the timing of it… it felt like it was aimed at me.\n\nAnd that’s the thing with those systems, they act like it’s all just computers and schedules, but when you’ve driven around enough places you start seeing how the numbers line up. So, yeah, even when my mood wasn’t so bad, that part was still happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you seen things that other people could not see?\n\nPatient: Seen things? Not so much like… I don’t know, shadows or people standing there, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll be sitting in my garage, everything shut off — no radio, no truck noise, nothing running — and I still hear dispatch clear as day. Like they’re calling my operator number, saying I’m dangerous, or saying security is coming.\n\nAnd the garage has this funny echo anyway, because of the concrete and the way sound comes through from the street, so I’ll check sometimes, but… it’s not like anybody’s actually there. So seeing, no, not really. Hearing, yeah, that’s the part.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices talking to each other about you, as if two or more people are discussing you when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes it sounds like dispatch, like more than one voice on the radio, but there’s no radio on. I’ll be sitting in my garage with everything shut off, and I still hear them clear, calling my operator number.\n\nThey’ll say I’m dangerous, or that security’s coming for me. It’s like they’re talking about me through the system. And the garage, it carries sound weird, you know, concrete walls and all that, but when everything’s off… I still hear it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that an outside force is controlling your thoughts or actions?\n\nPatient: Controlled… I don’t know if I’d say like a puppet, exactly. I still move my own hands, drive my own route, all that.\n\nBut the routing system at the warehouse, that thing isn’t just a computer to me anymore. It marks where I go, what scanner I use, what time I hit a dock. And then the messages come through the scanners like they’re nudging me, pushing me out, because I’m one of the old hands there.\n\nThey always say it’s just scheduling, just logistics, but I’ve been around long enough to know when something’s aimed at me. So… not controlling every thought maybe, but it’s steering things around me. Trying to force my hand.\n\nDoctor: During the past year and eight months, have you been using methamphetamine regularly?\n\nPatient: Meth? No, I don’t… I mean, I’ve seen what that stuff does to guys on the yard, especially on long shifts, everybody trying to stay awake and then they’re grinding their teeth by lunch.\n\nBut me using it regularly, no. That’s not something I’ve been doing. I drink coffee, too much probably, the gas station kind that tastes burnt, but meth isn’t my thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear a voice speaking your thoughts out loud as you think them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s my thoughts exactly, like word-for-word while I’m thinking them. It’s more… I’ll be sitting in my garage, everything shut off, no radio, no scanner, and I still hear dispatch voices clear as day.\n\nThey call my operator number, like they’re logged into the system or something, and then they’ll say I’m dangerous, or that security’s coming for me. The garage has this echo to it, concrete and tools and all that, so at first I thought maybe sound was carrying from somewhere… but there’s nothing on. It still comes through.\n\nDoctor: Have your thoughts ever felt so mixed up that you could not keep them organized?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mixed up, yes. It’s not just forgetting what I walked into a room for, like everybody does. At work especially, I’ll sort of freeze there and my hands… I’ll be holding them like they’re still on the forklift controls, even if I’m not on it.\n\nAnd I’ll get stuck saying the same safety phrase over and over, like, “check your clearance, check your clearance,” while somebody’s standing right in front of me trying to ask me something. Then I realize they’re looking at me strange, and I don’t know where the thread went.\n\nIt happens at home some too. I’ll be by the workbench or in the kitchen and just… lose the order of things. Like the steps won’t line up right. Then I’m back thinking about the yard, the docks, the old warning signs they used to have before they repainted everything—sorry, I know that’s not what you asked. But yes, my thoughts get mixed up enough that I can’t keep them straight.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
